    \makeatletter
    \newsavebox\pandoc@box
    \newcommand*\pandocbounded[1]{%
      \sbox\pandoc@box{#1}%
      % scaling factors for width and height
      \Gscale@div\@tempa\textheight{\dimexpr\ht\pandoc@box+\dp\pandoc@box\relax}%
      \Gscale@div\@tempb\linewidth{\wd\pandoc@box}%
      % select the smaller of both
      \ifdim\@tempb\p@<\@tempa\p@
        \let\@tempa\@tempb
      \fi
      % scaling accordingly (\@tempa < 1)
      \ifdim\@tempa\p@<\p@
        \scalebox{\@tempa}{\usebox\pandoc@box}%
      % scaling not needed, use as it is
      \else
        \usebox{\pandoc@box}%
      \fi
    }
    \makeatother

\def\PY@reset{\let\PY@it=\relax \let\PY@bf=\relax%
    \let\PY@ul=\relax \let\PY@tc=\relax%
    \let\PY@bc=\relax \let\PY@ff=\relax}
\def\PY@tok#1{\csname PY@tok@#1\endcsname}
\def\PY@toks#1+{\ifx\relax#1\empty\else%
    \PY@tok{#1}\expandafter\PY@toks\fi}
\def\PY@do#1{\PY@bc{\PY@tc{\PY@ul{%
    \PY@it{\PY@bf{\PY@ff{#1}}}}}}}
\def\PY#1#2{\PY@reset\PY@toks#1+\relax+\PY@do{#2}}

    % For linebreaks inside Verbatim environment from package fancyvrb.
    \makeatletter
        \newbox\Wrappedcontinuationbox
        \newbox\Wrappedvisiblespacebox
        \newcommand*\Wrappedvisiblespace {\textcolor{red}{\textvisiblespace}}
        \newcommand*\Wrappedcontinuationsymbol {\textcolor{red}{\llap{\tiny$\m@th\hookrightarrow$}}}
        \newcommand*\Wrappedcontinuationindent {3ex }
        \newcommand*\Wrappedafterbreak {\kern\Wrappedcontinuationindent\copy\Wrappedcontinuationbox}
        % Take advantage of the already applied Pygments mark-up to insert
        % potential linebreaks for TeX processing.
        %        {, <, #, %, $, ' and ": go to next line.
        %        _, }, ^, &, >, - and ~: stay at end of broken line.
        % Use of \textquotesingle for straight quote.
        \newcommand*\Wrappedbreaksatspecials {%
            \def\PYGZus{\discretionary{\char`\_}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\_}}%
            \def\PYGZob{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\{}{\char`\{}}%
            \def\PYGZcb{\discretionary{\char`\}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\}}}%
            \def\PYGZca{\discretionary{\char`\^}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\^}}%
            \def\PYGZam{\discretionary{\char`\&}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\&}}%
            \def\PYGZlt{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\<}{\char`\<}}%
            \def\PYGZgt{\discretionary{\char`\>}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\>}}%
            \def\PYGZsh{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\#}{\char`\#}}%
            \def\PYGZpc{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\%}{\char`\%}}%
            \def\PYGZdl{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\$}{\char`\$}}%
            \def\PYGZhy{\discretionary{\char`\-}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\-}}%
            \def\PYGZsq{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\textquotesingle}{\textquotesingle}}%
            \def\PYGZdq{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\"}{\char`\"}}%
            \def\PYGZti{\discretionary{\char`\~}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\~}}%
        }
        % Some characters . , ; ? ! / are not pygmentized.
        % This macro makes them "active" and they will insert potential linebreaks
        \newcommand*\Wrappedbreaksatpunct {%
            \lccode`\~`\.\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\.}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\.}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\,\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\,}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\,}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\;\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\;}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\;}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\:\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\:}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\:}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\?\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\?}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\?}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\!\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\!}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\!}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\/\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\/}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\/}}}%
            \catcode`\.\active
            \catcode`\,\active
            \catcode`\;\active
            \catcode`\:\active
            \catcode`\?\active
            \catcode`\!\active
            \catcode`\/\active
            \lccode`\~`\~
        }
    \makeatother

    \let\OriginalVerbatim=\Verbatim
    \makeatletter
    \renewcommand{\Verbatim}[1][1]{%
        %\parskip\z@skip
        \sbox\Wrappedcontinuationbox {\Wrappedcontinuationsymbol}%
        \sbox\Wrappedvisiblespacebox {\FV@SetupFont\Wrappedvisiblespace}%
        \def\FancyVerbFormatLine ##1{\hsize\linewidth
            \vtop{\raggedright\hyphenpenalty\z@\exhyphenpenalty\z@
                \doublehyphendemerits\z@\finalhyphendemerits\z@
                \strut ##1\strut}%
        }%
        % If the linebreak is at a space, the latter will be displayed as visible
        % space at end of first line, and a continuation symbol starts next line.
        % Stretch/shrink are however usually zero for typewriter font.
        \def\FV@Space {%
            \nobreak\hskip\z@ plus\fontdimen3\font minus\fontdimen4\font
            \discretionary{\copy\Wrappedvisiblespacebox}{\Wrappedafterbreak}
            {\kern\fontdimen2\font}%
        }%

        % Allow breaks at special characters using \PYG... macros.
        \Wrappedbreaksatspecials
        % Breaks at punctuation characters . , ; ? ! and / need catcode=\active
        \OriginalVerbatim[#1,codes*=\Wrappedbreaksatpunct]%
    }
    \makeatother

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Dataset
Information}}{Dataset Information}}\label{dataset-information}

This assignment uses the \textbf{Teacher Rating Dataset}
(\texttt{teacher\_ratings.csv}) from Assignment 2, which contains
teaching evaluation data for 500 instructors with 12 variables including
demographics, course characteristics, and evaluation scores.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

    \section{Teacher Rating Data Analysis - Assignment
6}\label{teacher-rating-data-analysis---assignment-6}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Assignment
Overview}}{Assignment Overview}}\label{assignment-overview}

This practical assignment focuses on advanced statistical hypothesis
testing and correlation analysis using the teacher rating dataset. The
assignment demonstrates the application of parametric and non-parametric
tests to answer key research questions about teaching evaluations.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Assignment
Objectives}}{Assignment Objectives}}\label{assignment-objectives}

This assignment focuses on comprehensive statistical hypothesis testing:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Independent Samples t-Test}: Examine gender differences in
  teaching evaluation scores
\item
  \textbf{One-Way ANOVA}: Compare beauty scores across different age
  groups
\item
  \textbf{Chi-Square Test of Independence}: Analyze association between
  tenure status and gender
\item
  \textbf{Correlation Analysis}: Investigate relationship between
  teaching evaluation and beauty scores
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Key Questions
Addressed}}{Key Questions Addressed}}\label{key-questions-addressed}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Question 1 (5 marks)}: Does gender affect teaching evaluation
  rates? (t-Test)
\item
  \textbf{Question 2 (5 marks)}: Does beauty score for instructors
  differ by age? (ANOVA)
\item
  \textbf{Question 3 (5 marks)}: Is there an association between tenure
  and gender? (Chi-square)
\item
  \textbf{Question 4 (5 marks)}: Is teaching evaluation score correlated
  with beauty score? (Correlation)
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Key Learning
Objectives}}{Key Learning Objectives}}\label{key-learning-objectives}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Statistical hypothesis testing (t-tests, ANOVA, chi-square)
\item
  Assumption checking for parametric tests
\item
  Effect size calculation and interpretation
\item
  Correlation analysis and significance testing
\item
  Data visualization for statistical results
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Question 1: T-Test - Does Gender Affect Teaching Evaluation
Rates?}\label{question-1-t-test---does-gender-affect-teaching-evaluation-rates}

\subsection{Problem Statement}\label{problem-statement}

Using the teachers' rating dataset, does gender affect teaching
evaluation rates?

\subsection{Approach}\label{approach}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  State hypotheses (null and alternative)
\item
  Check assumptions for independent samples t-test:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Independence of observations
  \item
    Normality of distributions in each group
  \item
    Homogeneity of variances (Levene's test)
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Perform independent samples t-test
\item
  Calculate effect size (Cohen's d)
\item
  Visualize the distributions
\item
  Interpret results
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

    \subsection{Statistical Theory: Independent Samples
t-Test}\label{statistical-theory-independent-samples-t-test}

\subsubsection{What is a t-Test?}\label{what-is-a-t-test}

The independent samples t-test is a parametric statistical test used to
compare the means of two independent groups. It determines whether there
is a statistically significant difference between the mean values of a
continuous variable for two groups.

\subsubsection{Mathematical Formulation}\label{mathematical-formulation}

The t-statistic is calculated as:

\[t = \frac{\bar{X}_1 - \bar{X}_2}{s_p \sqrt{\frac{1}{n_1} + \frac{1}{n_2}}}\]

Where: - \(\bar{X}_1, \bar{X}_2\) = sample means of groups 1 and 2 -
\(s_p\) = pooled standard deviation - \(n_1, n_2\) = sample sizes of
groups 1 and 2

The pooled standard deviation is:

\[s_p = \sqrt{\frac{(n_1-1)s_1^2 + (n_2-1)s_2^2}{n_1 + n_2 - 2}}\]

\subsubsection{Degrees of Freedom}\label{degrees-of-freedom}

For equal variances: \(df = n_1 + n_2 - 2\)

\subsubsection{Assumptions}\label{assumptions}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Independence}: Observations in each group are independent
\item
  \textbf{Normality}: Data in each group are approximately normally
  distributed
\item
  \textbf{Homogeneity of variances}: Variances are equal across groups
  (can use Welch's t-test if violated)
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Effect Size: Cohen's d}\label{effect-size-cohens-d}

Cohen's d measures the standardized difference between two means:

\[d = \frac{\bar{X}_1 - \bar{X}_2}{s_p}\]

\textbf{Interpretation Guidelines}: - \textbar d\textbar{} \textless{}
0.2: negligible effect - 0.2 ≤ \textbar d\textbar{} \textless{} 0.5:
small effect - 0.5 ≤ \textbar d\textbar{} \textless{} 0.8: medium effect
- \textbar d\textbar{} ≥ 0.8: large effect

\subsubsection{When to Use Welch's
t-test}\label{when-to-use-welchs-t-test}

If variances are unequal (Levene's test p \textless{} 0.05), use Welch's
t-test which does not assume equal variances:

\[t = \frac{\bar{X}_1 - \bar{X}_2}{\sqrt{\frac{s_1^2}{n_1} + \frac{s_2^2}{n_2}}}\]

with adjusted degrees of freedom using the Welch-Satterthwaite equation.

    \subsubsection{Step 1: State Hypotheses}\label{step-1-state-hypotheses}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{5}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{QUESTION 1: Does Gender Affect Teaching Evaluation Rates?}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Hypotheses:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{H₀ (Null): μ\PYZus{}male = μ\PYZus{}female (No difference in mean evaluation scores between genders)}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{H₁ (Alternative): μ\PYZus{}male ≠ μ\PYZus{}female (There is a difference in mean evaluation scores)}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

    \subsubsection{Step 2: Prepare Data and Check for Missing
Values}\label{step-2-prepare-data-and-check-for-missing-values}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{7}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Filter data and remove NaN values}
\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}clean} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{gender}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{eval\PYZus{}score}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{dropna}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Separate data by gender}
\PY{n}{male\PYZus{}eval} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}clean}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}clean}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{gender}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{==} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{male}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{eval\PYZus{}score}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{female\PYZus{}eval} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}clean}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}clean}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{gender}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{==} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{female}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{eval\PYZus{}score}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}

    \subsubsection{Step 3: Check
Assumptions}\label{step-3-check-assumptions}

\paragraph{Assumption 1: Independence}\label{assumption-1-independence}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Each teacher's evaluation is independent (satisfied by study design)
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Assumption 2: Normality}\label{assumption-2-normality}

    \subsubsection{Understanding Normality
Tests}\label{understanding-normality-tests}

\textbf{Shapiro-Wilk Test}: Tests the null hypothesis that data comes
from a normal distribution. - H₀: Data is normally distributed - H₁:
Data is not normally distributed - If p \textgreater{} 0.05: Fail to
reject H₀ (data appears normal) - If p \textless{} 0.05: Reject H₀ (data
deviates from normality)

\textbf{Q-Q Plots}: Visual method to assess normality. If points fall
approximately on a straight line, data is approximately normal.

    \subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Comprehensive Assumption Checking
Process}}{Comprehensive Assumption Checking Process}}\label{comprehensive-assumption-checking-process}

Statistical tests have assumptions that must be checked before
interpretation.

\textbf{1. Normality Assumption}

\textbf{Shapiro-Wilk Test:}

Tests whether data come from a normal distribution.

\textbf{Test Statistic:}
\[W = \frac{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n} a_i x_{(i)}\right)^2}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_i - \bar{x})^2}\]

where: - \(x_{(i)}\) are ordered sample values - \(a_i\) are weights
(from expected values of order statistics) - \(W\) ranges from 0 to 1

\textbf{Interpretation:} - \(W \approx 1\): Data is normally distributed
- \(W << 1\): Data deviates from normality - \(p > 0.05\): Fail to
reject normality (assume normal) - \(p \leq 0.05\): Reject normality
(not normal)

\textbf{Q-Q Plot Visual Check:}

Plots: - \textbf{x-axis:} Theoretical quantiles (from normal
distribution) - \textbf{y-axis:} Sample quantiles (from data)

\textbf{Interpretation:} - \textbf{Points on diagonal line:} Perfectly
normal - \textbf{S-shaped curve:} Heavy-tailed or skewed distribution -
\textbf{Points above line at edges:} Heavy-tailed - \textbf{Points below
line at edges:} Light-tailed

\textbf{What to do if normality violated:} 1. \textbf{Transform data:}
log, square root, Box-Cox transformation 2. \textbf{Use non-parametric
test:} Mann-Whitney U instead of t-test 3. \textbf{Rely on Central Limit
Theorem:} With n \textgreater{} 30, means are approximately normal

\textbf{2. Homogeneity of Variance (Homoscedasticity)}

\textbf{Levene's Test:}

Tests whether variances are equal across groups.

\textbf{Test Statistic:}
\[F = \frac{(N-k)\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_i(\bar{Z}_i - \bar{Z})^2}{(k-1)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{n_i}(Z_{ij} - \bar{Z}_i)^2}\]

where \(Z_{ij} = |X_{ij} - \tilde{X}_i|\) (absolute deviation from group
median)

\textbf{Interpretation:} - \(p > 0.05\): Fail to reject equal variances
(assume homogeneity) - \(p \leq 0.05\): Reject equal variances
(heterogeneity present)

\textbf{Visual Check - Boxplots:} - Similar box heights across groups →
Equal variances - Very different box heights → Unequal variances

\textbf{What to do if homogeneity violated:} 1. \textbf{Use Welch's
t-test:} Doesn't assume equal variances 2. \textbf{Use Brown-Forsythe
test:} Robust version of Levene's 3. \textbf{Transform data:} Sometimes
stabilizes variance

\textbf{Variance Ratio Rule of Thumb:}
\[\frac{s_{max}^2}{s_{min}^2} < 4\]

If ratio \textgreater{} 4, consider equal variance assumption violated.

\textbf{3. Independence Assumption}

\textbf{What it means:} - Each observation is independent of others - No
correlation between residuals - Random sampling without replacement (or
with replacement)

\textbf{How to check:} - \textbf{Study design:} Was sampling truly
random? - \textbf{Durbin-Watson test:} For time-series data (checks
autocorrelation) - \textbf{Scatter plot of residuals:} No patterns
should appear

\textbf{What to do if violated:} 1. \textbf{Repeated measures ANOVA:}
For within-subjects designs 2. \textbf{Mixed effects models:} For
hierarchical/clustered data 3. \textbf{Time series analysis:} For
temporally correlated data

\textbf{4. Linearity Assumption (for correlation)}

\textbf{What it means:} - Relationship between X and Y is linear
(straight line) - Pearson's r only detects \textbf{linear} relationships

\textbf{How to check:} - \textbf{Scatter plot:} Points should follow
straight line pattern - \textbf{Residual plot:} Random scatter around
zero line

\textbf{What to do if violated:} 1. \textbf{Transform variables:}
log(X), √X, X² 2. \textbf{Use Spearman's rho:} Detects monotonic (not
just linear) relationships 3. \textbf{Polynomial regression:} Fits
curved relationships

\textbf{5. No Outliers Assumption}

\textbf{What they are:} - Extreme values far from the rest of the data -
Can greatly influence means, correlations, regression

\textbf{Detection methods:}

\textbf{Z-Score Method:} \[z_i = \frac{x_i - \bar{x}}{s}\]

Outlier if \(|z_i| > 3\) (or sometimes 2.5)

\textbf{IQR Method:} \[\text{Lower fence} = Q_1 - 1.5 \times IQR\]
\[\text{Upper fence} = Q_3 + 1.5 \times IQR\]

Outlier if outside fences.

\textbf{What to do with outliers:} 1. \textbf{Check for errors:} Data
entry mistakes, measurement errors 2. \textbf{Analyze with/without:}
Compare results both ways 3. \textbf{Use robust methods:} Median instead
of mean, Spearman instead of Pearson 4. \textbf{Transform data:} Log
transformation can reduce outlier impact 5. \textbf{Report them:}
Transparent reporting of outlier handling

\textbf{Never arbitrarily delete outliers just to get significant
results!}

\textbf{Summary Decision Tree:}

\begin{verbatim}
START
 ↓
Check Normality (Shapiro-Wilk + Q-Q plot)
 ├─ Normal? YES → Check Homogeneity (Levene's test)
 │                  ├─ Equal variances? YES → Use standard t-test/ANOVA
 │                  └─ Equal variances? NO → Use Welch's t-test
 │
 └─ Normal? NO → Transform data or use non-parametric tests
                   (Mann-Whitney U, Kruskal-Wallis, Spearman's rho)
\end{verbatim}

    \paragraph{Assumption 3: Homogeneity of
Variances}\label{assumption-3-homogeneity-of-variances}

    \subsubsection{Understanding Homogeneity of
Variances}\label{understanding-homogeneity-of-variances}

\textbf{Levene's Test}: Tests whether variances are equal across groups.
- H₀: σ₁² = σ₂² (variances are equal) - H₁: σ₁² ≠ σ₂² (variances are
different) - If p \textgreater{} 0.05: Use standard t-test
(equal\_var=True) - If p \textless{} 0.05: Use Welch's t-test
(equal\_var=False)

This is important because the pooled standard deviation formula assumes
equal variances.

    \subsubsection{Step 4: Perform Independent Samples
t-Test}\label{step-4-perform-independent-samples-t-test}

    \subsubsection{Understanding the t-Test
Calculation}\label{understanding-the-t-test-calculation}

The t-statistic measures how many standard errors the difference between
group means is from zero.

\textbf{Step-by-step calculation}: 1. Calculate group means:
\(\bar{X}_1\), \(\bar{X}_2\) 2. Calculate group standard deviations:
\(s_1\), \(s_2\) 3. Calculate pooled standard deviation: \(s_p\) 4.
Calculate standard error:
\(SE = s_p \sqrt{\frac{1}{n_1} + \frac{1}{n_2}}\) 5. Calculate
t-statistic: \(t = \frac{\bar{X}_1 - \bar{X}_2}{SE}\) 6. Find p-value
from t-distribution with \(df = n_1 + n_2 - 2\)

\textbf{Interpretation}: - Large \textbar t\textbar{} value: Groups are
far apart relative to variability - Small \textbar t\textbar{} value:
Groups are close together relative to variability - p-value \textless{}
0.05: Significant difference exists (reject H₀)

    \subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Step-by-Step t-Test Calculation
Process}}{Step-by-Step t-Test Calculation Process}}\label{step-by-step-t-test-calculation-process}

Let's walk through the manual calculation of an independent samples
t-test:

\textbf{Step 1: Calculate Group Means}
\[\bar{X}_1 = \frac{1}{n_1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1} X_{1i} \quad \text{and} \quad \bar{X}_2 = \frac{1}{n_2}\sum_{i=1}^{n_2} X_{2i}\]

\textbf{Step 2: Calculate Group Standard Deviations}
\[s_1 = \sqrt{\frac{1}{n_1-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_1}(X_{1i} - \bar{X}_1)^2} \quad \text{and} \quad s_2 = \sqrt{\frac{1}{n_2-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_2}(X_{2i} - \bar{X}_2)^2}\]

\textbf{Step 3: Calculate Pooled Variance (if equal variances assumed)}
\[s_p^2 = \frac{(n_1-1)s_1^2 + (n_2-1)s_2^2}{n_1 + n_2 - 2}\]

This is a \textbf{weighted average} of the two sample variances, where
the weights are the degrees of freedom.

\textbf{Step 4: Calculate Standard Error of Difference}
\[SE_{\bar{X}_1 - \bar{X}_2} = s_p\sqrt{\frac{1}{n_1} + \frac{1}{n_2}}\]

\textbf{Step 5: Calculate t-Statistic}
\[t = \frac{(\bar{X}_1 - \bar{X}_2) - (\mu_1 - \mu_2)}{SE_{\bar{X}_1 - \bar{X}_2}}\]

Under H₀: \(\mu_1 = \mu_2\), so \((\mu_1 - \mu_2) = 0\):

\[t = \frac{\bar{X}_1 - \bar{X}_2}{s_p\sqrt{\frac{1}{n_1} + \frac{1}{n_2}}}\]

\textbf{Step 6: Determine Degrees of Freedom} \[df = n_1 + n_2 - 2\]

\textbf{Step 7: Find Critical Value and P-value} - Look up
\(t_{\alpha/2, df}\) from t-distribution table (for two-tailed test) -
Calculate p-value: \(P(|T| \geq |t_{obs}|)\) where \(T \sim t_{df}\)

\textbf{Step 8: Make Decision} - If \(|t_{obs}| > t_{critical}\) or
\(p < \alpha: Reject H₀\) - If \(|t_{obs}| \leq t_{critical}\) or
\(p \geq \alpha: Fail to reject H₀\)

\textbf{Numerical Example:} - Male mean = 4.0, Female mean = 4.2 -
\(s_1 = 0.5\), \(s_2 = 0.6\) - \(n_1 = 250\), \(n_2 = 250\)

\[s_p^2 = \frac{(250-1)(0.5)^2 + (250-1)(0.6)^2}{250 + 250 - 2} = \frac{62.25 + 89.64}{498} = 0.305\]

\[s_p = \sqrt{0.305} = 0.552\]

\[SE = 0.552\sqrt{\frac{1}{250} + \frac{1}{250}} = 0.552 \times 0.0894 = 0.0494\]

\[t = \frac{4.2 - 4.0}{0.0494} = \frac{0.2}{0.0494} = 4.05\]

With \(df = 498\) and \(\alpha = 0.05\), \(t_{critical} = 1.96\)

Since \$\textbar4.05\textbar{} \textgreater{} 1.96, we reject H₀.

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  p\PYZhy{}value: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}levene}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.6f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  Conclusion: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Equal variances (p \PYZgt{} 0.05)}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{if}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}levene}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{\PYZgt{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+m+mf}{0.05}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{else}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Unequal variances (p \PYZlt{} 0.05)}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Variance Ratio (Male/Female): }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{variance\PYZus{}ratio}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.4f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{conclusion} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Homogeneity assumption satisfied}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}} \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{variance\PYZus{}ratio} \PY{o}{\PYZlt{}} \PY{l+m+mi}{4} \PY{o+ow}{and} \PY{n}{variance\PYZus{}ratio} \PY{o}{\PYZgt{}} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.25} \PY{k}{else} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Consider using Welch}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{s t\PYZhy{}test}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}

    \subsubsection{Step 5: Calculate Effect Size (Cohen's
d)}\label{step-5-calculate-effect-size-cohens-d}

    \subsubsection{Understanding Cohen's d}\label{understanding-cohens-d}

Cohen's d is the \textbf{effect size} measure for t-tests. While
p-values tell us if a difference exists, Cohen's d tells us how large
that difference is.

\textbf{Formula}: \[d = \frac{\bar{X}_1 - \bar{X}_2}{s_p}\]

\textbf{Interpretation}: - It expresses the mean difference in standard
deviation units - Example: d = 0.5 means groups differ by half a
standard deviation - d = 0.8 means groups differ by 0.8 standard
deviations (large effect)

\textbf{Why Effect Size Matters}: - Statistical significance (p-value)
depends on sample size - Large samples can find tiny, meaningless
differences significant - Effect size tells us if the difference is
practically important - Always report both p-value and effect size

    \subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Detailed Interpretation of Effect
Sizes}}{Detailed Interpretation of Effect Sizes}}\label{detailed-interpretation-of-effect-sizes}

Effect sizes tell us the \textbf{practical significance} of our
findings, independent of sample size.

\textbf{Cohen's d (for t-tests):}

\[d = \frac{\bar{X}_1 - \bar{X}_2}{s_p}\]

where \(s_p\) is the pooled standard deviation.

\textbf{Interpretation Guidelines:} - \textbar d\textbar{} = 0.2:
\textbf{Small effect} - Detectable only statistically - Example: 0.2 SD
difference between groups - Real-world: Noticeable to researchers, not
obvious to casual observers

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbar d\textbar{} = 0.5: \textbf{Medium effect} - Visible to the
  naked eye

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Example: 0.5 SD difference between groups
  \item
    Real-world: Obvious difference when groups are compared
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbar d\textbar{} = 0.8: \textbf{Large effect} - Substantial
  practical importance

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Example: 0.8 SD difference between groups
  \item
    Real-world: Difference is unmistakable and practically significant
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\textbf{What does d = 0.5 mean practically?} - The means differ by half
a standard deviation - There's approximately 67\% non-overlap between
distributions - If you randomly select one person from each group,
there's a 64\% chance the person from the higher-scoring group will have
a higher score

\textbf{Eta-squared (η²) for ANOVA:}

\[\eta^2 = \frac{SSB}{SST} = \frac{\text{Variance Between Groups}}{\text{Total Variance}}\]

\textbf{Interpretation:} - η² = 0.01: \textbf{Small effect} - Group
membership explains 1\% of variance - η² = 0.06: \textbf{Medium effect}
- Group membership explains 6\% of variance - η² = 0.14: \textbf{Large
effect} - Group membership explains 14\% of variance

\textbf{Example:} η² = 0.08 means: - 8\% of variance in the outcome is
due to group differences - 92\% of variance is due to other factors
(individual differences, measurement error) - This is considered a
\textbf{medium effect}

\textbf{Cramér's V (for chi-square):}

\[V = \sqrt{\frac{\chi^2}{N \times \min(r-1, c-1)}}\]

\textbf{For 2×2 tables:} - V = 0.10: \textbf{Small effect} - Weak
association - V = 0.30: \textbf{Medium effect} - Moderate association -
V = 0.50: \textbf{Large effect} - Strong association

\textbf{Example:} V = 0.15 means: - Weak to moderate association between
variables - Knowing one variable provides limited information about the
other - The relationship exists but isn't strong enough for practical
prediction

\textbf{R² (Coefficient of Determination):}

\[R^2 = r^2\]

\textbf{Interpretation:} - R² = 0.01: \textbf{1\% of variance explained}
- Very weak relationship - R² = 0.09: \textbf{9\% of variance explained}
- Weak relationship\\
- R² = 0.25: \textbf{25\% of variance explained} - Moderate relationship
- R² = 0.64: \textbf{64\% of variance explained} - Strong relationship

\textbf{Example:} r = 0.6, R² = 0.36 means: - 36\% of variance in Y is
predictable from X - 64\% of variance is due to other factors - This
represents a \textbf{moderate to strong} relationship

\textbf{Why Effect Sizes Matter:} 1. \textbf{Sample Size Independence:}
P-values depend on sample size; effect sizes don't 2. \textbf{Practical
Significance:} Statistical significance ≠ practical importance 3.
\textbf{Comparability:} Can compare effects across studies 4.
\textbf{Meta-Analysis:} Essential for combining studies 5. \textbf{Power
Analysis:} Used to determine required sample sizes

\textbf{Common Mistake:} Relying only on p-values without considering
effect sizes can lead to: - \textbf{Significant but trivial findings}
(large sample, small effect) - \textbf{Practically important but
non-significant findings} (small sample, large effect)

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{INDEPENDENT SAMPLES T\PYZhy{}TEST}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Standard t\PYZhy{}test (equal variances)}
\PY{n}{t\PYZus{}stat}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{p\PYZus{}value} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{ttest\PYZus{}ind}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{male\PYZus{}eval}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{female\PYZus{}eval}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Welch\PYZsq{}s t\PYZhy{}test (unequal variances \PYZhy{} more robust)}
\PY{n}{t\PYZus{}stat\PYZus{}welch}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{p\PYZus{}value\PYZus{}welch} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{ttest\PYZus{}ind}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{male\PYZus{}eval}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{female\PYZus{}eval}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{equal\PYZus{}var}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{False}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Standard t\PYZhy{}test (equal variances assumed):}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  t\PYZhy{}statistic: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{t\PYZus{}stat}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.6f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  p\PYZhy{}value: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}value}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.6f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  Degrees of freedom: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{male\PYZus{}eval}\PY{p}{)}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{+}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{female\PYZus{}eval}\PY{p}{)}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+m+mi}{2}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Welch}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{s t\PYZhy{}test (unequal variances, more robust):}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  t\PYZhy{}statistic: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{t\PYZus{}stat\PYZus{}welch}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.6f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  p\PYZhy{}value: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}value\PYZus{}welch}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.6f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Decision}
\PY{n}{alpha} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.05}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{STATISTICAL DECISION}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Significance level: α = }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{alpha}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{p\PYZus{}value\PYZus{}welch} \PY{o}{\PYZlt{}} \PY{n}{alpha}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Decision: REJECT H₀ (p = }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}value\PYZus{}welch}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.4f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ \PYZlt{} }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{alpha}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{)}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Conclusion: There IS a statistically significant difference in mean evaluation scores between genders.}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{k}{else}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Decision: FAIL TO REJECT H₀ (p = }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}value\PYZus{}welch}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.4f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ ≥ }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{alpha}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{)}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Conclusion: There is NO statistically significant difference in mean evaluation scores between genders.}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Question 2: ANOVA - Does Beauty Score Differ by
Age?}\label{question-2-anova---does-beauty-score-differ-by-age}

\subsection{Problem Statement}\label{problem-statement}

Using the teachers' rating dataset, does beauty score for instructors
differ by age?

\subsection{Approach}\label{approach}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Create age groups for ANOVA analysis
\item
  State hypotheses
\item
  Check assumptions:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Independence of observations
  \item
    Normality within groups
  \item
    Homogeneity of variances
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Perform one-way ANOVA
\item
  If significant, perform post-hoc tests
\item
  Calculate effect size (eta-squared)
\item
  Visualize results
\item
  Interpret findings
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

    \subsection{Statistical Theory: One-Way
ANOVA}\label{statistical-theory-one-way-anova}

\subsubsection{What is ANOVA?}\label{what-is-anova}

\textbf{AN}alysis \textbf{O}f \textbf{VA}riance (ANOVA) is used to
compare means across \textbf{three or more} independent groups. It tests
whether at least one group mean differs from the others.

\subsubsection{Why Not Multiple
t-Tests?}\label{why-not-multiple-t-tests}

Performing multiple t-tests increases \textbf{Type I error rate} (false
positives): - 3 groups = 3 comparisons: α increases from 0.05 to
\textasciitilde0.14 - 4 groups = 6 comparisons: α increases to
\textasciitilde0.26 - ANOVA controls the overall α at 0.05

\subsubsection{Mathematical Formulation}\label{mathematical-formulation}

ANOVA partitions total variance into: 1. \textbf{Between-group variance}
(SSB): Variation due to group differences 2. \textbf{Within-group
variance} (SSW): Variation within groups (error)

\[F = \frac{MS_B}{MS_W} = \frac{SS_B / df_B}{SS_W / df_W}\]

Where: - \(SS_B = \sum_{i=1}^{k} n_i(\bar{X}_i - \bar{X}_{grand})^2\)
(between-group sum of squares) -
\(SS_W = \sum_{i=1}^{k} \sum_{j=1}^{n_i} (X_{ij} - \bar{X}_i)^2\)
(within-group sum of squares) - \(df_B = k - 1\) (k = number of groups)
- \(df_W = N - k\) (N = total sample size)

\subsubsection{F-Distribution}\label{f-distribution}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  F-statistic follows an F-distribution with \((df_B, df_W)\) degrees of
  freedom
\item
  Large F: Between-group variance \textgreater\textgreater{}
  Within-group variance → groups differ
\item
  Small F: Between-group variance ≈ Within-group variance → groups
  similar
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Assumptions}\label{assumptions}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Independence}: Observations are independent
\item
  \textbf{Normality}: Data in each group is approximately normal
\item
  \textbf{Homogeneity of variances}: Variances are equal across all
  groups
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Effect Size: Eta-Squared
(η²)}\label{effect-size-eta-squared-ux3b7uxb2}

Measures proportion of total variance explained by group membership:

\[\eta^2 = \frac{SSB}{SS_T} = \frac{SSB}{SS_B + SS_W}\]

\textbf{Interpretation}: - η² \textless{} 0.01: negligible effect - 0.01
≤ η² \textless{} 0.06: small effect - 0.06 ≤ η² \textless{} 0.14: medium
effect - η² ≥ 0.14: large effect

\subsubsection{Post-Hoc Tests}\label{post-hoc-tests}

If ANOVA is significant, perform \textbf{post-hoc pairwise comparisons}
to identify which specific groups differ: - \textbf{Bonferroni
correction}: Adjust α for multiple comparisons (α\_adjusted = α / number
of comparisons) - \textbf{Tukey's HSD}: Controls family-wise error rate
- \textbf{Scheffé's test}: Most conservative

\subsubsection{Non-Parametric
Alternative}\label{non-parametric-alternative}

\textbf{Kruskal-Wallis test}: Used when normality assumption is
violated. Tests whether groups have different distributions (not just
means).

    \subsubsection{Step 1: Create Age
Groups}\label{step-1-create-age-groups}

    \subsubsection{Step 2: State Hypotheses}\label{step-2-state-hypotheses}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{20}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Check assumptions for ANOVA}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ASSUMPTION CHECKS}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Normality for each group}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{1. Normality Test (Shapiro\PYZhy{}Wilk):}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{name}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{data} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Below 40}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{below\PYZus{}40}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{40\PYZhy{}50}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{age\PYZus{}40\PYZus{}50}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Above 50}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{above\PYZus{}50}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{stat}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{p} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{shapiro}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{data}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{name}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{: W = }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{stat}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.6f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{, p = }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{p}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.6f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ \PYZhy{} }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Normal}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{if}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{p}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{\PYZgt{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+m+mf}{0.05}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{else}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Not normal}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Homogeneity of variances}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{2. Homogeneity of Variances (Levene}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{s Test):}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{stat\PYZus{}lev}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{p\PYZus{}lev} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{levene}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{below\PYZus{}40}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{age\PYZus{}40\PYZus{}50}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{above\PYZus{}50}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  F = }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{stat\PYZus{}lev}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.6f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{, p = }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}lev}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.6f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ \PYZhy{} }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Equal variances}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{if}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}lev}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{\PYZgt{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+m+mf}{0.05}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{else}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Unequal variances}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \subsubsection{Step 4: Perform One-Way
ANOVA}\label{step-4-perform-one-way-anova}

    \subsubsection{Understanding the ANOVA
F-Statistic}\label{understanding-the-anova-f-statistic}

The F-statistic is the ratio of \textbf{systematic variance} to
\textbf{random variance}:

\[F = \frac{\text{Variance between groups}}{\text{Variance within groups}} = \frac{\text{Signal}}{\text{Noise}}\]

\textbf{Intuition}: - If groups have different means, between-group
variance will be large → Large F - If groups overlap, between-group
variance will be small → Small F - F ≈ 1: Group means are similar (H₀
likely true) - F \textgreater\textgreater{} 1: At least one group
differs (H₀ likely false)

\textbf{ANOVA Table Structure}:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Source & SS & df & MS & F \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Between & SSB & k-1 & SSB/(k-1) & MSB/MSW \\
Within & SSW & N-k & SSW/(N-k) & - \\
Total & SST & N-1 & - & - \\
\end{longtable}

    \subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Step-by-Step ANOVA Calculation
Process}}{Step-by-Step ANOVA Calculation Process}}\label{step-by-step-anova-calculation-process}

Let's manually calculate a one-way ANOVA:

\textbf{Given Data Structure:} - Group 1 (Below 40): \(n_1\)
observations, values \(X_{11}, X_{12}, ..., X_{1n_1}\) - Group 2
(40-50): \(n_2\) observations, values \(X_{21}, X_{22}, ..., X_{2n_2}\)
- Group 3 (Above 50): \(n_3\) observations, values
\(X_{31}, X_{32}, ..., X_{3n_3}\) - Total observations:
\(N = n_1 + n_2 + n_3\)

\textbf{Step 1: Calculate Group Means}
\[\bar{X}_j = \frac{1}{n_j}\sum_{i=1}^{n_j} X_{ji} \quad \text{for } j = 1, 2, 3\]

\textbf{Step 2: Calculate Grand Mean}
\[\bar{X}_{\cdot\cdot} = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{i=1}^{n_j} X_{ji} = \frac{n_1\bar{X}_1 + n_2\bar{X}_2 + n_3\bar{X}_3}{N}\]

\textbf{Step 3: Calculate Sum of Squares Between Groups (SSB)}
\[SSB = \sum_{j=1}^{3} n_j(\bar{X}_j - \bar{X}_{\cdot\cdot})^2\]

This measures \textbf{variation between group means}.

\textbf{Step 4: Calculate Sum of Squares Within Groups (SSW)}
\[SSW = \sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{i=1}^{n_j}(X_{ji} - \bar{X}_j)^2\]

This measures \textbf{variation within each group} (pooled).

\textbf{Step 5: Calculate Total Sum of Squares (SST)}
\[SST = \sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{i=1}^{n_j}(X_{ji} - \bar{X}_{\cdot\cdot})^2\]

\textbf{Verification:} \(SST = SSB + SSW\)

\textbf{Step 6: Calculate Degrees of Freedom} - Between groups:
\(df_B = k - 1 = 3 - 1 = 2\) (where \(k\) = number of groups) - Within
groups: \(df_W = N - k = N - 3\) - Total: \(df_T = N - 1\)

\textbf{Step 7: Calculate Mean Squares}
\[MSB = \frac{SSB}{df_B} \quad \text{(Between-group variance)}\]

\[MSW = \frac{SSW}{df_W} \quad \text{(Within-group variance, pooled)}\]

\textbf{Step 8: Calculate F-Statistic} \[F = \frac{MSB}{MSW}\]

\textbf{Interpretation:} - \(F \approx 1\): Group means are similar
(variation between ≈ variation within) - \(F >> 1\): Group means differ
significantly (variation between \textgreater\textgreater{} variation
within)

\textbf{Step 9: Find Critical Value and P-value} - Compare \(F_{obs}\)
to \(F_{\alpha, df_B, df_W}\) from F-distribution table - Calculate
p-value: \(P(F_{df_B, df_W} \geq F_{obs})\)

\textbf{Step 10: Calculate Effect Size (Eta-squared)}
\[\eta^2 = \frac{SSB}{SST}\]

This is the \textbf{proportion of total variance} explained by group
membership.

\textbf{Numerical Example:} Suppose we have: - Group 1 (Below 40):
\(n_1 = 150\), \(\bar{X}_1 = 3.8\), \(s_1^2 = 0.4\) - Group 2 (40-50):
\(n_2 = 200\), \(\bar{X}_2 = 4.0\), \(s_2^2 = 0.5\) - Group 3 (Above
50): \(n_3 = 150\), \(\bar{X}_3 = 4.2\), \(s_3^2 = 0.45\) - Grand mean:
\(\bar{X}_{\cdot\cdot} = \frac{150(3.8) + 200(4.0) + 150(4.2)}{500} = 4.0\)

\textbf{Calculate SSB:}
\[SSB = 150(3.8-4.0)^2 + 200(4.0-4.0)^2 + 150(4.2-4.0)^2\]
\[SSB = 150(0.04) + 0 + 150(0.04) = 6 + 0 + 6 = 12\]

\textbf{Calculate SSW:}
\[SSW = (n_1-1)s_1^2 + (n_2-1)s_2^2 + (n_3-1)s_3^2\]
\[SSW = 149(0.4) + 199(0.5) + 149(0.45) = 59.6 + 99.5 + 67.05 = 226.15\]

\textbf{Calculate F:} \[MSB = \frac{12}{2} = 6.0\]
\[MSW = \frac{226.15}{497} = 0.455\] \[F = \frac{6.0}{0.455} = 13.19\]

With \(df_1 = 2, df_2 = 497\), and \(\alpha = 0.05\),
\(F_{critical} \approx 3.0\)

Since \$13.19 \textgreater{} 3.0, we reject H₀.

\textbf{Effect size:}
\[\eta^2 = \frac{12}{12 + 226.15} = \frac{12}{238.15} = 0.050 \text{ (5%)}
\]

Interpretation: 5\% of variance in beauty scores is explained by age
group.

Post-hoc tests (if ANOVA is significant): - Bonferroni correction:
α\_adjusted = α / number of comparisons - Tukey's HSD: Controls
family-wise error rate - Scheffé's test: Most conservative

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{24}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Post\PYZhy{}hoc tests: Bonferroni pairwise comparisons}
\PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{p\PYZus{}value\PYZus{}anova} \PY{o}{\PYZlt{}} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.05}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{POST\PYZhy{}HOC TESTS (Bonferroni\PYZhy{}corrected pairwise comparisons)}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}

    \PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{scipy}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{stats}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{ttest\PYZus{}ind}

    \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Number of comparisons}
    \PY{n}{num\PYZus{}comparisons} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mi}{3}
    \PY{n}{alpha\PYZus{}bonf} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{alpha} \PY{o}{/} \PY{n}{num\PYZus{}comparisons}

    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Adjusted significance level (Bonferroni): α = }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{alpha\PYZus{}bonf}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.4f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Pairwise Comparisons:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{comparisons} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}

    \subsubsection{Understanding Post-Hoc
Tests}\label{understanding-post-hoc-tests}

When ANOVA is significant (p \textless{} 0.05), we know \textbf{at least
one group differs}, but we don't know \textbf{which specific pairs}
differ.

\textbf{Bonferroni Correction}: - Problem: Multiple comparisons inflate
Type I error - Solution: Divide α by number of comparisons - For 3
groups (3 comparisons): α\_adjusted = 0.05/3 = 0.0167 - Conservative but
protects against false positives

\textbf{Example with 3 Groups (Young, Middle, Senior)}: 1. Young vs
Middle 2. Young vs Senior\\
3. Middle vs Senior

Total comparisons = \(\binom{k}{2} = \frac{k(k-1)}{2}\) where k = number
of groups

\textbf{Interpretation}: - Compare each pairwise p-value to α\_adjusted
- Only reject H₀ if p \textless{} α\_adjusted - This controls the
\textbf{family-wise error rate}

Research Question:
Is there a significant association between gender and tenure status?

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
HYPOTHESES

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{p\PYZhy{}value: p = }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}value\PYZus{}chi}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.6f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}} \PY{o}{+} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{expected\PYZus{}df} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{DataFrame}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{expected}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{index}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{observed}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{index}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{columns}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{observed}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{columns}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{expected\PYZus{}df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{round}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{2}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Check assumptions}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}} \PY{o}{+} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ASSUMPTION CHECK}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{min\PYZus{}expected} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{expected}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{min}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{cells\PYZus{}below\PYZus{}5} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{expected} \PY{o}{\PYZlt{}} \PY{l+m+mi}{5}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sum}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{total\PYZus{}cells} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{expected}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{size}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Minimum expected frequency: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{min\PYZus{}expected}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.2f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Number of cells with expected frequency \PYZlt{} 5: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{cells\PYZus{}below\PYZus{}5}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ out of }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{total\PYZus{}cells}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Percentage of cells \PYZlt{} 5: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{cells\PYZus{}below\PYZus{}5}\PY{o}{/}\PY{n}{total\PYZus{}cells}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{100}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.1f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZpc{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Assumption: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{SATISFIED}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{if}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{min\PYZus{}expected}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{\PYZgt{}}\PY{o}{=}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+m+mi}{5}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o+ow}{and}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{cells\PYZus{}below\PYZus{}5}\PY{o}{/}\PY{n}{total\PYZus{}cells}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{\PYZlt{}}\PY{o}{=}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+m+mf}{0.20}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{else}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{VIOLATED}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  (All expected frequencies should be ≥ 5, or at most 20}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZpc{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ \PYZlt{} 5)}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Statistical decision}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}} \PY{o}{+} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{STATISTICAL DECISION}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{α = 0.05}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{p\PYZhy{}value = }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}value\PYZus{}chi}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.6f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Decision: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Reject H₀}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{if}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}value\PYZus{}chi}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{\PYZlt{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+m+mf}{0.05}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{else}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Fail to reject H₀}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Conclusion: There }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{IS}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{if}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}value\PYZus{}chi}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{\PYZlt{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+m+mf}{0.05}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{else}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{IS NO}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ significant association between tenure status and gender}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

    \begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Question 3: Chi-Square Test - Is There an Association Between
Tenure and
Gender?}\label{question-3-chi-square-test---is-there-an-association-between-tenure-and-gender}

\subsection{Problem Statement}\label{problem-statement}

Using the teachers' rating dataset, is there an association between
tenure and gender?

\subsection{Approach}\label{approach}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  State hypotheses
\item
  Create contingency table
\item
  Check assumptions:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Independence of observations
  \item
    Expected frequencies (all cells ≥ 5)
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Perform chi-square test of independence
\item
  Calculate effect size (Cramér's V)
\item
  Visualize the relationship
\item
  Interpret results
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

    \subsection{Statistical Theory: Chi-Square Test of
Independence}\label{statistical-theory-chi-square-test-of-independence}

\subsubsection{What is Chi-Square Test?}\label{what-is-chi-square-test}

The chi-square (χ²) test of independence determines whether two
categorical variables are associated (related) or independent.

\subsubsection{Mathematical Formulation}\label{mathematical-formulation}

The chi-square statistic measures how much observed frequencies deviate
from expected frequencies:

\[\chi^2 = \sum_{i=1}^{r} \sum_{j=1}^{c} \frac{(O_{ij} - E_{ij})^2}{E_{ij}}\]

Where: - \(O_{ij}\) = observed frequency in cell (i, j) - \(E_{ij}\) =
expected frequency in cell (i, j) - r = number of rows - c = number of
columns

\subsubsection{Expected Frequencies}\label{expected-frequencies}

Under the null hypothesis (independence), expected frequencies are
calculated as:

\[E_{ij} = \frac{(\text{Row}_i \text{ total}) \times (\text{Column}_j \text{ total})}{\text{Grand total}}\]

\subsubsection{Degrees of Freedom}\label{degrees-of-freedom}

\[df = (r - 1) \times (c - 1)\]

For a 2×2 table: \(df = (2-1) \times (2-1) = 1\)

\subsubsection{Chi-Square Distribution}\label{chi-square-distribution}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  χ² values are always positive (squared differences)
\item
  Right-skewed distribution
\item
  Large χ²: Observed differs greatly from expected → association exists
\item
  Small χ²: Observed close to expected → variables independent
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Assumptions}\label{assumptions}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Independence}: Each observation belongs to only one cell
\item
  \textbf{Expected frequencies}: All expected frequencies ≥ 5

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    If violated, consider Fisher's exact test (for 2×2 tables)
  \item
    Or combine categories to increase cell counts
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Sample size}: Sufficient sample size (typically n ≥ 20)
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Effect Size: Cramér's V}\label{effect-size-cramuxe9rs-v}

Measures strength of association for categorical variables:

\[V = \sqrt{\frac{\chi^2}{n \times \min(r-1, c-1)}}\]

\textbf{Interpretation Guidelines (2×2 table)}: - V \textless{} 0.1:
negligible association - 0.1 ≤ V \textless{} 0.3: small association -
0.3 ≤ V \textless{} 0.5: medium association - V ≥ 0.5: large association

\subsubsection{Odds Ratio (for 2×2
Tables)}\label{odds-ratio-for-22-tables}

Quantifies association between two binary variables:

For contingency table: \textbar{} \textbar{} Column 1 \textbar{} Column
2 \textbar{}
\textbar-------\textbar----------\textbar----------\textbar{} \textbar{}
Row 1 \textbar{} a \textbar{} b \textbar{} \textbar{} Row 2 \textbar{} c
\textbar{} d \textbar{}

\[OR = \frac{ad}{bc}\]

\textbf{Interpretation}: - OR = 1: No association - OR \textgreater{} 1:
Row 1 has higher odds of Column 2 - OR \textless{} 1: Row 1 has lower
odds of Column 2 - Example: OR = 2.5 means Row 1 has 2.5 times the odds
of Column 2 compared to Row 2

    \subsubsection{Step 1: State Hypotheses}\label{step-1-state-hypotheses}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{QUESTION 3: Is There an Association Between Tenure and Gender?}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}

    \subsubsection{Step 2: Create Contingency
Table}\label{step-2-create-contingency-table}

    \subsubsection{Understanding Contingency
Tables}\label{understanding-contingency-tables}

A \textbf{contingency table} (or cross-tabulation) displays the
frequency distribution of two categorical variables.

\textbf{Structure}:

\begin{verbatim}
              Variable 2
            Category A  Category B  Total
Variable 1  
Category X      n₁₁        n₁₂      n₁.
Category Y      n₂₁        n₂₂      n₂.
Total           n.₁        n.₂       n
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Row Percentages}: Show distribution of Variable 2 within each
level of Variable 1 \textbf{Column Percentages}: Show distribution of
Variable 1 within each level of Variable 2

\textbf{Example Interpretation}: If 60\% of males are tenured but only
40\% of females are tenured, this suggests a potential association
between gender and tenure status (to be tested with χ²).

\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]

                                \PY{n}{margins}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{margins\PYZus{}name}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Total}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{row\PYZus{}pct} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{crosstab}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}q3}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{gender}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}q3}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{tenured}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{normalize}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{index}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{*} \PY{l+m+mi}{100}

\PY{n}{col\PYZus{}pct} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{crosstab}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}q3}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{gender}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}q3}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{tenured}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{normalize}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{columns}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{*} \PY{l+m+mi}{100}
\PY{n}{col\PYZus{}pct}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{columns} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Not Tenured (}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZpc{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{)}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Tenured (}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZpc{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{)}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{display}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{col\PYZus{}pct}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{round}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{2}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \subsubsection{Step 3: Check Assumptions and Perform Chi-Square
Test}\label{step-3-check-assumptions-and-perform-chi-square-test}

    \subsubsection{Understanding the Chi-Square
Calculation}\label{understanding-the-chi-square-calculation}

\textbf{Step-by-Step Process}:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Create observed frequency table} (what we actually see in
  data)
\item
  \textbf{Calculate expected frequencies} assuming independence:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    For each cell:
    \(E = \frac{\text{row total} \times \text{column total}}{\text{grand total}}\)
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Calculate chi-square statistic}:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    For each cell: \(\frac{(O - E)^2}{E}\)
  \item
    Sum all cells: \(\chi^2 = \sum \frac{(O - E)^2}{E}\)
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Find p-value} from chi-square distribution with appropriate df
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Intuition}: - If variables are independent: O ≈ E → small χ² -
If variables are associated: O differs from E → large χ² - Each term
\((O-E)^2/E\) measures contribution of that cell to overall association

    \subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\textbf{Step-by-Step Chi-Square
Calculation
Process}}{Step-by-Step Chi-Square Calculation Process}}\label{step-by-step-chi-square-calculation-process}

Let's manually calculate chi-square for a 2×2 contingency table:

\textbf{Given Contingency Table:}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
& Non-Tenured & Tenured & Row Total \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Male & \(O_{11}\) = a & \(O_{12}\) = b & \(R_1 = a + b\) \\
Female & \(O_{21}\) = c & \(O_{22}\) = d & \(R_2 = c + d\) \\
\textbf{Column Total} & \(C_1 = a + c\) & \(C_2 = b + d\) &
\(N = a+b+c+d\) \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{Step 1: Calculate Expected Frequencies}

For each cell \((i,j)\): \[E_{ij} = \frac{R_i \times C_j}{N}\]

Specifically:
\[E_{11} = \frac{(a+b)(a+c)}{N} \quad E_{12} = \frac{(a+b)(b+d)}{N}\]

\[E_{21} = \frac{(c+d)(a+c)}{N} \quad E_{22} = \frac{(c+d)(b+d)}{N}\]

\textbf{Why this formula?} Under independence:
\(P(\text{Male and Non-Tenured}) = P(\text{Male}) \times P(\text{Non-Tenured})\)

\[E_{11} = N \times \frac{R_1}{N} \times \frac{C_1}{N} = \frac{R_1 \times C_1}{N}\]

\textbf{Step 2: Calculate Chi-Square Components}

For each cell:
\[\text{Component}_{ij} = \frac{(O_{ij} - E_{ij})^2}{E_{ij}}\]

This measures \textbf{squared standardized deviation} from expectation.

\textbf{Step 3: Sum All Components}
\[\chi^2 = \sum_{i=1}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2} \frac{(O_{ij} - E_{ij})^2}{E_{ij}}\]

\[\chi^2 = \frac{(a - E_{11})^2}{E_{11}} + \frac{(b - E_{12})^2}{E_{12}} + \frac{(c - E_{21})^2}{E_{21}} + \frac{(d - E_{22})^2}{E_{22}}\]

\textbf{Step 4: Calculate Degrees of Freedom}
\[df = (r-1)(c-1) = (2-1)(2-1) = 1\]

where \(r\) = number of rows, \(c\) = number of columns

\textbf{Step 5: Find Critical Value and P-value} - Compare
\(\chi^2_{obs}\) to \(\chi^2_{\alpha, df}\) from chi-square distribution
table - Calculate p-value: \(P(\chi^2_{df} \geq \chi^2_{obs})\)

\textbf{Step 6: Calculate Effect Size (Cramér's V)}
\[V = \sqrt{\frac{\chi^2}{n \times \min(r-1, c-1)}} = \sqrt{\frac{\chi^2}{n}}\]

Using our example:
\[\chi^2 = \frac{500[(120 \times 150) - (130 \times 100)]^2}{250 \times 250 \times 220 \times 280}\]

\[\chi^2 = \frac{500[18000 - 13000]^2}{250 \times 250 \times 220 \times 280} = \frac{500 \times 25000000}{3850000000} = 3.246\]

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{ }{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Calculate Cramér\PYZsq{}s V (effect size)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{EFFECT SIZE: CRAMÉR}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{S V}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{n} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{observed}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sum}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sum}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

================================================================================
ODDS RATIO (OR)
================================================================================

Contingency table cells:
                  0          1
    female    a=128        b=144
      male    c= 70        d=158

Odds Ratio (OR) = (a × d) / (b × c)
OR = (128 × 158) / (144 × 70) = 2.0063

95\% Confidence Interval: [1.3878, 2.9007]

Interpretation:
  female are 2.01 times more likely to have
  0 status compared to male

    \begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Question 4: Correlation - Is Teaching Evaluation Score
Correlated with Beauty
Score?}\label{question-4-correlation---is-teaching-evaluation-score-correlated-with-beauty-score}

\subsection{Problem Statement}\label{problem-statement}

Using the teachers' rating dataset, is teaching evaluation score
correlated with beauty score?

\subsection{Approach}\label{approach}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  State hypotheses
\item
  Check assumptions:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Linearity of relationship
  \item
    Bivariate normality
  \item
    Absence of outliers
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Calculate Pearson correlation coefficient
\item
  Test significance of correlation
\item
  Calculate Spearman correlation (non-parametric alternative)
\item
  Visualize the relationship
\item
  Interpret results and explain variance
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

    \subsection{Statistical Theory: Correlation
Analysis}\label{statistical-theory-correlation-analysis}

\subsubsection{What is Correlation?}\label{what-is-correlation}

Correlation measures the \textbf{strength and direction} of a linear
relationship between two continuous variables.

\subsubsection{Pearson Correlation Coefficient
(r)}\label{pearson-correlation-coefficient-r}

\textbf{Formula}:
\[r = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_i - \bar{X})(Y_i - \bar{Y})}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_i - \bar{X})^2} \sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_i - \bar{Y})^2}}\]

Or equivalently: \[r = \frac{Cov(X, Y)}{s_X \times s_Y}\]

Where: - \(Cov(X, Y)\) = covariance between X and Y - \(s_X, s_Y\) =
standard deviations of X and Y

\subsubsection{Properties of r}\label{properties-of-r}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Range}: -1 ≤ r ≤ +1
\item
  \textbf{Direction}:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    r \textgreater{} 0: Positive relationship (X ↑, Y ↑)
  \item
    r \textless{} 0: Negative relationship (X ↑, Y ↓)
  \item
    r = 0: No linear relationship
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Strength}:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbar r\textbar{} = 1: Perfect linear relationship
  \item
    \textbar r\textbar{} = 0: No linear relationship
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Unitless}: Not affected by scale or units
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Interpretation
Guidelines}\label{interpretation-guidelines}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbar r\textbar{} \textless{} 0.1: Negligible correlation
\item
  0.1 ≤ \textbar r\textbar{} \textless{} 0.3: Weak correlation
\item
  0.3 ≤ \textbar r\textbar{} \textless{} 0.5: Moderate correlation
\item
  0.5 ≤ \textbar r\textbar{} \textless{} 0.7: Strong correlation
\item
  \textbar r\textbar{} ≥ 0.7: Very strong correlation
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Coefficient of Determination
(R²)}\label{coefficient-of-determination-ruxb2}

\[R^2 = r^2\]

\textbf{Interpretation}: Proportion of variance in Y that is predictable
from X (and vice versa)

\textbf{Example}: r = 0.6 → R² = 0.36 - 36\% of variance in Y is
explained by X - 64\% of variance is due to other factors

\subsubsection{Assumptions for Pearson
Correlation}\label{assumptions-for-pearson-correlation}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Linearity}: Relationship between variables is linear
\item
  \textbf{Bivariate normality}: Both variables are approximately
  normally distributed
\item
  \textbf{Homoscedasticity}: Variance is constant across range
\item
  \textbf{No outliers}: Extreme values can distort correlation
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Spearman's Rank Correlation
(ρ)}\label{spearmans-rank-correlation-ux3c1}

\textbf{Non-parametric alternative} when: - Data is ordinal (ranked) -
Relationship is monotonic but not linear - Normality assumption is
violated - Outliers are present

\textbf{Formula}: Same as Pearson's r, but applied to \textbf{ranks}
instead of raw values

\subsubsection{Hypothesis Test for
Correlation}\label{hypothesis-test-for-correlation}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  H₀: ρ = 0 (no correlation in population)
\item
  H₁: ρ ≠ 0 (correlation exists in population)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Test statistic:} \[t = \frac{r\sqrt{n-2}}{\sqrt{1-r^2}}\]

with df = n - 2

\subsubsection{Important Notes}\label{important-notes}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Correlation ≠ Causation}: r measures association, not
  cause-effect
\item
  \textbf{Linear only}: Pearson's r only detects linear relationships
\item
  \textbf{Sensitive to outliers}: A few extreme points can greatly
  affect r
\item
  \textbf{Sample size matters}: Larger samples needed to detect weak
  correlations
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Confidence Interval for
r}\label{confidence-interval-for-r}

Use \textbf{Fisher's z-transformation}:
\[z = \frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{1+r}{1-r}\right)\]

Then calculate CI for z and transform back to r.

    \subsubsection{Step 1: State Hypotheses}\label{step-1-state-hypotheses}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{38}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Question 4: Pearson correlation}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{QUESTION 4: PEARSON CORRELATION}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Research Question:}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Is there a significant correlation between beauty score and evaluation score?}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}} \PY{o}{+} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{HYPOTHESES}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Null Hypothesis (H₀): ρ = 0}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  No linear correlation between beauty score and evaluation score}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Alternative Hypothesis (H₁): ρ ≠ 0}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  There is a linear correlation between beauty score and evaluation score}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Test Type: Two\PYZhy{}tailed Pearson correlation test}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Significance Level: α = 0.05}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \subsubsection{Step 3: Check
Assumptions}\label{step-3-check-assumptions}

\paragraph{Assumption 1: Linearity (visual inspection via scatter
plot)}\label{assumption-1-linearity-visual-inspection-via-scatter-plot}

\paragraph{Assumption 2: Bivariate
Normality}\label{assumption-2-bivariate-normality}

    \subsubsection{Understanding Correlation vs
Causation}\label{understanding-correlation-vs-causation}

\textbf{Critical Distinction}: - \textbf{Correlation}: Two variables
change together - \textbf{Causation}: One variable directly causes
changes in another

\textbf{Why Correlation ≠ Causation}: 1. \textbf{Confounding variables}:
A third variable may influence both - Example: Ice cream sales and
drowning deaths correlate, but heat causes both 2. \textbf{Reverse
causality}: Y might cause X instead of X causing Y 3.
\textbf{Coincidence}: Random patterns in finite data

\textbf{Requirements for Causation}: 1. Temporal precedence (cause
precedes effect) 2. Covariation (variables are correlated) 3. No
plausible alternative explanations 4. Usually requires
\textbf{experimental} design (not just observational data)

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  p\PYZhy{}value = }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}eval}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.6f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  Conclusion: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Normally distributed (p \PYZgt{} 0.05)}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{if}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}eval}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{\PYZgt{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+m+mf}{0.05}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{else}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Not normally distributed (p \PYZlt{} 0.05)}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}} \PY{o}{+} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{ }{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Perform Pearson correlation test}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{scipy}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{stats}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{pearsonr}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{PEARSON CORRELATION TEST}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{r}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{p\PYZus{}value\PYZus{}corr} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pearsonr}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df\PYZus{}corr}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{beauty}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{df\PYZus{}corr}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{eval\PYZus{}score}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Pearson Correlation Coefficient: r = }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{r}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.6f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{p\PYZhy{}value (two\PYZhy{}tailed): p = }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}value\PYZus{}corr}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.6f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Interpret strength of correlation}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}} \PY{o}{+} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{INTERPRETATION OF CORRELATION STRENGTH}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{*}\PY{l+m+mi}{80}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{abs\PYZus{}r} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n+nb}{abs}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{r}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{abs\PYZus{}r} \PY{o}{\PYZlt{}} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.10}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{strength} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Negligible}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}
\PY{k}{elif} \PY{n}{abs\PYZus{}r} \PY{o}{\PYZlt{}} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.30}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{strength} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Weak}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}
\PY{k}{elif} \PY{n}{abs\PYZus{}r} \PY{o}{\PYZlt{}} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.50}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{strength} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Moderate}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}
\PY{k}{elif} \PY{n}{abs\PYZus{}r} \PY{o}{\PYZlt{}} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.70}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{strength} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Strong}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}
\PY{k}{else}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{strength} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Very Strong}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}

\PY{n}{direction} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{positive}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}} \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{r} \PY{o}{\PYZgt{}} \PY{l+m+mi}{0} \PY{k}{else} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{negative}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Correlation Strength: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{strength}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Guidelines (Cohen, 1988):}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  Negligible: |r| \PYZlt{} 0.10}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  Weak: 0.10 ≤ |r| \PYZlt{} 0.30}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  Moderate: 0.30 ≤ |r| \PYZlt{} 0.50}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  Strong: 0.50 ≤ |r| \PYZlt{} 0.70}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{  Very Strong: |r| ≥ 0.70}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Calculate coefficient of determination (R²)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{is explained by beauty score (and vice versa)}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{Decision: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Reject H₀}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{if}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n}{p\PYZus{}value\PYZus{}corr}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{o}{\PYZlt{}}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+m+mf}{0.05}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{else}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Fail to reject H₀}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{k}{else}\PY{p}{:}
